• Disturbance events → increase → CWD inputs 
Knowledge Card 7.2.2 – Methods for estimating volume of logs and 
snags 
Estimating the volume of a CWD fragment assumes circular cross-sections. Three 
methods are commonly used. Standard volume functions relate diameter and height to 
stem volume for commercially important species; this ignores stump volumes and works 
best for relatively intact stems. Sectioning divides a log into segments and calculates 
volume for each using formulas such as Huber’s, Smalian’s and Newton’s; Newton’s 
formula is unbiased for cylinders, cones and paraboloids. The sample length and midpoint 
diameter may suffice for partial logs, while Smalian’s formula is suitable for whole stems 
with divisions into ≤5 sections. Taper functions describe trunk shape for some species; 
integrating the taper function yields volume. When logs are irregularly shaped, water 
displacement provides an alternative by immersing the fragment and measuring volume. 
Standing snags require measuring diameter at breast height (dbh), base and top 
diameters for broken boles and height. For stumps, height and midpoint or base and top 
diameters are measured. 
Entities: volume functions; sectioning; taper function; Huber’s method; Smalian’s method; 
Newton’s method; water displacement; standing snags; dbh; stump; species 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Volume functions → relate diameter and height to → stem volume 
• Sectioning → divides logs into → segments 
• Newton’s method → provides → unbiased volume estimates 
• Taper function → describes → trunk shape 
• Water displacement → measures → volume of irregular logs 
• Standing snags → require → measuring dbh, base and top diameters 
• Stumps → require → measuring height and midpoint diameter 
Knowledge Card 7.2.3 – Survey methods for CWD in forest stands 
Objective surveys quantify CWD across forest stands using probability sampling and the 
Horvitz–Thompson estimator. Key challenges involve treating fragments near stand 
boundaries; techniques such as the mirage method reflect sampling units across 
boundaries to ensure equal inclusion probability. Major survey methods include sample 
plot inventory, strip (belt) surveying and line intercept sampling. Randomly located circular 
or rectangular plots capture logs and snags; nested plots sample different diameter 
classes, but boundary fragments require clear inclusion rules. Strip surveys lay long, thin 
transects across stands and sample all CWD fragments within the strip. Line intercept 
sampling counts downed logs crossed by a transect; the probability of inclusion is 

--- PAGE 24 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
proportional to log length and is useful only for lying debris. Each method has biases; 
point-relascope sampling may provide efficient estimates but methods vary in time 
efficiency and accuracy. 
Entities: sample plot inventory; strip survey; line intercept sampling; Horvitz–
Thompson estimator; mirage method; nested plots; CWD fragments; boundary; 
probability sampling; transects 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Sample plot inventory → uses → circular or rectangular plots 
• Strip survey → uses → long narrow transects 
• Line intercept sampling → counts → logs crossed by transects 
• Mirage method → reflects → sampling units across boundaries 
• Horvitz–Thompson estimator → estimates → population totals 
• Nested plots → sample → different diameter classes 
• Boundary fragments → require → inclusion rules 
Knowledge Card 7.2.4 – Decay classification, wood density and decay 
rate 
CWD decays through progressive changes in solidity and surface characteristics. Logs 
and snags are often placed into three to five decay classes based on bark cover, presence 
of twigs and roots, degree of support, and colonization by bryophytes and fungi. Table 7.2 
lists five classes: Class I (intact bark, twigs and solid wood), Class II (some bark loss and 
early decay), Class III (bark absent, log still supports weight, moss and roots present), 
Class IV (log cannot support its weight, fragment breaks when kicked) and Class V (soft, 
powdery mound). Penetrometers classify logs based on rod penetration depth into slightly, 
moderately and advanced decay. Wood density is measured by collecting samples from 
each decay class, determining volume via dimensions or water displacement, then 
oven-drying to constant mass. Decay rate can be modelled using a single-exponential 
decay function (Yt = Y₀ e⁻ᵏᵗ), where k is the decay constant. Half-life (t₀.₅) and 95 % 
decomposition time (t₀.₉₅) express time to 50 % and 95 % mass loss. 
Entities: decay class; wood density; penetrometer; log samples; oven-drying; 
exponential decay model; decay rate constant; half-life; bryophytes; fungi 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Decay class → is defined by → bark cover and structural integrity 
• Penetrometer → measures → decay class via penetration depth 

--- PAGE 25 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
• Wood density → is measured by → water displacement and oven-drying 
• Exponential decay model → expresses → mass loss over time 
• Decay rate constant → determines → half-life of CWD 
• Bryophytes and fungi → colonize → decayed logs 
Structured Summary – Coarse woody debris 
CWD encompasses large dead woody material such as logs, snags and roots that provide 
essential habitat and nutrient storage functions. Sampling begins by recognising that CWD 
inputs arise from episodic mortality events and branch breakage; objective methods such 
as permanent plots and disturbance reconstructions capture these inputs. Volume 
estimation assumes roughly circular cross-sections and relies on standard volume 
functions, sectioning formulas or taper functions, with water displacement used for 
irregular fragments. Survey methods treat boundary fragments carefully and employ 
sample plot inventories, strip surveys and line intercept sampling, each with its own 
biases. Assessment of CWD also involves classifying decay stages based on external 
traits and measuring wood density via sample collection, water displacement and 
oven-drying. Single-exponential models describe decomposition dynamics, enabling 
predictions of half-life and remaining mass. Together, these methods allow researchers to 
quantify the ecological contributions of CWD, monitor changes in dead wood abundance 
and evaluate sustainable forest management practices. 
7.3 Vertical stand structure 
Knowledge Card 7.3.1 – Definition and significance of vertical structure 
Vertical stand structure describes the bottom-to-top configuration of above-ground 
vegetation, encompassing variation in canopy density, tree size, branching patterns, leaf 
distribution, understorey density and the presence of snags and fallen trees. This structural 
complexity influences light gradients, microclimate, habitat availability and species 
interactions, making it a key component of forest biodiversity. The foliage height diversity 
(FHD) index quantifies the arrangement of foliage across vertical layers; it sums over 
layers the proportion of foliage (p_i) multiplied by its log, providing a measure analogous to 
Shannon’s diversity index. MacArthur and MacArthur originally found FHD correlated with 
bird diversity, yet subsequent studies show inconsistent relationships across taxa and 
difficulties comparing strata definitions. Despite these limitations, vertical structure metrics 
remain widely used to assess habitat heterogeneity and to guide management practices 
aimed at retaining multi-layered canopies. 
Entities: vertical stand structure; canopy density; tree size; branching patterns; 
foliage height diversity (FHD); p_i (proportion of foliage); bird diversity; microclimate 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Vertical stand structure → includes → canopy density 

--- PAGE 26 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
• Vertical stand structure → includes → tree size variation 
• Vertical stand structure → influences → microclimate 
• FHD → summarizes → arrangement of foliage across strata 
• Higher FHD → can correlate with → bird diversity 
• FHD → depends on → p_i values for each layer 
• Branching patterns → affect → habitat availability 
Knowledge Card 7.3.2 – Arc-based method and cover index 
Ferris-Kaan et al. (1998) proposed an arc-based method to quantify vertical structure by 
establishing a 10 m radius around the observer, defining a 20 m arc across which canopy 
cover is assessed. Foliage is divided into four height bands: S1 (field layer 10 cm–1.9 m), 
S2 (shrub layer 2–5 m), S3 (lower canopy 5.1–15 m) and S4 (upper canopy 15.1–20 m). 
Within each band, percentage cover of each species is visually estimated, and multiple 
assessments are averaged over permanent plots. A cover index (CI) converts these cover 
estimates into a unified measure by weighting each stratum’s cover by its depth 
(CI = 1.9 S1 + 3 S2 + 10 S3 + 5 S4). The CI ranges from 0 to 1990 and emphasises 
contributions of mid and upper canopy layers. Estimating canopy cover is challenging; 
observers often traverse the arc and visually integrate horizontal plane cover. This method 
provides a consistent, repeatable way of comparing stands, although subjectivity and 
observer bias remain significant limitations. 
Entities: arc-based method; cover index (CI); S1 (field layer); S2 (shrub layer); S3 (lower 
canopy); S4 (upper canopy); canopy cover; permanent sample plots 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Arc-based method → divides → canopy into S1, S2, S3, S4 
• S1 → covers → 10 cm–1.9 m height 
• S2 → covers → 2–5 m height 
• S3 → covers → 5.1–15 m height 
• S4 → covers → 15.1–20 m height 
• Cover index → weights → cover by stratum depth 
• Permanent sample plots → aggregate → cover estimates

Knowledge Card 7.3.3 – Stratification indices and critiques 
Describing canopy stratification is challenging because strata boundaries are often 
arbitrary and vary among studies. Ashton and Hall (1992) proposed a stratification index 
calculated as the ratio of plant biomass in the densest height class to that in the least 
dense height class, allowing comparison among stands but not specifying the number of 
strata. Latham et al. (1998) introduced TSTRAT, an algorithm that sorts trees by height and 
live crown ratio and groups individuals into strata based on a competition cut-off point. 
Baker and Wilson (2000) critiqued TSTRAT, suggesting it often overestimates stratum 
number and proposed a moving-average method comparing tree height and height to base 
of live crown. More fundamentally, Parker and Brown (2000) argued that focusing on 
discrete layers can obscure ecological gradients; instead, indices should capture 
continuous variations in light, moisture and other resources. No single index has universal 
acceptance, and McElhinny et al. (2005) emphasised guidelines for developing structural 
complexity indices tailored to management goals. 
Entities: stratification index; TSTRAT algorithm; moving-average method; canopy strata; 
plant biomass; height class; competition cut-off point; ecological gradients 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Stratification index → compares → densest and least dense height classes 
• TSTRAT → groups → trees into strata based on height and crown ratio 
• Moving-average method → evaluates → tree height relative to mean height to base 
of live crown 
• Parker and Brown → argue that → canopy stratification may obscure ecological 
gradients 
• Ecological gradients → influence → light and resource availability 
• Indices → guide → management of structural complexity 
Structured Summary – Vertical stand structure 
Vertical stand structure integrates the diversity of tree sizes, branch architecture and 
foliage distribution from the forest floor to the upper canopy. Foliage height diversity (FHD) 
offers a conceptual measure of how foliage is distributed among layers, but its relation to 
biodiversity varies across taxa, reflecting differences in foraging and nesting niches. Field 
methods, such as Ferris-Kaan’s arc-based approach, subdivide the canopy into discrete 
height bands and compute a cover index weighted by layer depth, facilitating quantitative 
comparisons among stands. Alternative stratification indices, including the Ashton & Hall 
ratio, TSTRAT algorithm and moving-average methods, attempt to define strata objectively 
but may overestimate the number of layers or depend on arbitrary cut-offs. Critics 
advocate focusing on continuous ecological gradients rather than discrete layers to 
capture functional variation in light, moisture and resources. Because no single metric 
captures all aspects of vertical complexity, researchers develop indices tailored to specific 

--- PAGE 28 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
biodiversity objectives, and management guidelines emphasise maintaining multilayered 
structures that support diverse plant and animal communities. 
7.4 Forest fragmentation 
Knowledge Card 7.4.1 – Habitat loss versus fragmentation 
Forest fragmentation refers to breaking apart habitat into smaller patches, often 
accompanied by habitat loss. Fahrig (2003) distinguishes between habitat loss, which 
almost always reduces biodiversity, and fragmentation per se, which can have positive, 
negative or neutral effects. Many studies conflate these processes, making it difficult to 
discern the effects of patch arrangement. Habitat loss reduces the amount of suitable area 
for species, leading to smaller populations and greater extinction risk. Fragmentation alters 
patch size, shape and isolation, affecting dispersal, gene flow and community interactions. 
Experimental designs must separate the amount of habitat from its spatial configuration to 
accurately assess fragmentation impacts. Recognising this distinction is critical for 
conservation planning; focusing solely on fragmentation may misdirect efforts away from 
preserving habitat extent. However, fragmentation can modify ecological processes via 
edge effects, changes in predator–prey dynamics and altered movement patterns, which 
are addressed in later sections. 
Entities: forest fragmentation; habitat loss; habitat fragmentation; biodiversity; patch size; 
patch isolation; dispersal; gene flow; edge effects; Fahrig (2003) 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Habitat loss → reduces → biodiversity 
• Fragmentation per se → changes → patch size, shape and isolation 
• Fragmentation → affects → dispersal and gene flow 
• Studies → often conflate → habitat loss and fragmentation 
• Conservation planning → must distinguish between → habitat loss and 
fragmentation 
• Edge effects → arise from → fragmentation 
Knowledge Card 7.4.2 – Design issues in fragmentation studies 
Many fragmentation studies compare biodiversity in ‘reference’ continuous landscapes to 
one fragmented landscape, resulting in sample sizes of two and limiting statistical 
inference. Such designs cannot examine gradients of fragmentation because each 
landscape is either continuous or fragmented. Furthermore, measures of fragmentation—
number of patches, total edge length, mean nearest-neighbour distance—are often 
confounded with habitat amount. Patch-scale studies provide data from multiple patches 
but only one landscape, precluding landscape-level inference. To overcome these issues, 

--- PAGE 29 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
studies should compare multiple landscapes across a gradient of habitat amounts and 
degrees of fragmentation. Sampling units (e.g., landscapes) need to be independent, and 
metrics should be calculated at the landscape scale to avoid pseudoreplication. 
Recognising the limitations of typical designs encourages more robust experimental and 
statistical approaches for disentangling fragmentation effects. 
Entities: reference landscape; fragmented landscape; sample size; fragmentation metrics; 
number of patches; edge length; nearest-neighbour distance; patch-scale study; 
landscape-scale study; gradient 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Reference landscape → is compared to → fragmented landscape 
• Small sample size → limits → statistical inference 
• Fragmentation metrics → are confounded with → habitat amount 
• Patch-scale study → samples → multiple patches within one landscape 
• Landscape-scale study → compares → multiple landscapes 
• Studies → should evaluate → gradients of habitat amount and fragmentation 
Knowledge Card 7.4.3 – Measuring fragmentation independently of 
habitat area 
To isolate fragmentation effects, researchers can statistically control for habitat amount. 
Villard et al. (1999) measured the number of forest patches, total edge length, mean 
nearest-neighbour distance and forest cover across 33 landscapes; they then regressed 
the fragmentation variables against forest cover and used residuals as fragmentation 
metrics. Alternatively, experimental landscapes can manipulate patch arrangement while 
keeping habitat amount constant, enabling comparison between one large patch and 
several smaller patches. Studies separating habitat loss and fragmentation often find 
habitat amount has a more pronounced effect on biodiversity than fragmentation. 
Consequently, conservation efforts should prioritise maintaining adequate habitat area 
rather than merely increasing connectivity; attempts to minimise fragmentation may be 
ineffectual if habitat amount remains low. Nonetheless, fragmentation can still influence 
ecological processes via edge effects and should be considered alongside habitat quantity. 
Entities: regression approach; residual fragmentation metrics; experimental landscape; 
large patch; small patches; biodiversity; forest cover; conservation 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Regression approach → controls for → habitat amount 

--- PAGE 30 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
• Residual metrics → represent → fragmentation independent of area 
• Experimental landscapes → compare → one large patch and several small patches 
• Habitat amount → has greater effect on → biodiversity than fragmentation 
• Conservation efforts → should prioritise → maintaining habitat area 
• Edge effects → result from → fragmentation 
Structured Summary – Forest fragmentation 
Fragmentation research distinguishes between the outright loss of habitat and changes in 
its spatial configuration. While habitat loss invariably reduces biodiversity, fragmentation 
per se can have variable effects depending on species’ dispersal abilities, edge tolerance 
and habitat requirements. Many early studies relied on comparisons of a single continuous 
landscape with one fragmented landscape, confounding fragmentation with habitat amount 
and limiting inference. Contemporary approaches call for sampling multiple independent 
landscapes along gradients of habitat area and fragmentation; statistical methods such as 
regression residuals or manipulative experiments can control for habitat amount, 
generating metrics of fragmentation independent of area. These studies often find that the 
quantity of habitat exerts stronger influence on biodiversity than patch arrangement, 
suggesting conservation efforts should first ensure sufficient habitat area. Nonetheless, 
fragmentation alters edge length, patch isolation and shape, leading to edge effects and 
modified ecological interactions. A nuanced understanding of fragmentation therefore 
requires integrating habitat quantity, spatial configuration and the processes operating at 
patch edges. 
7.5 Edge characteristics and effects 
Knowledge Card 7.5.1 – Defining edges and processes influenced 
Edges occur where forest patches meet other habitat or land-cover types; their 
characteristics differ from interior conditions and can influence dispersal, migration, 
predation, competition and gene flow. Ecological studies often assess variation across 
edges by placing plots or transects perpendicular to the boundary. Remote sensing can 
also describe edge length, width, shape and structural attributes. Edge length and contrast 
modulate flows of energy, materials and organisms; high-contrast (‘hard’) edges may act 
as barriers, whereas ‘soft’ edges with successional vegetation are more permeable. Edges 
influence species distributions, altering microclimate and resource availability and 
generating edge-related species assemblages. Understanding these processes is crucial 
for conservation planning because fragmentation increases the proportion of edge relative 
to interior habitat, affecting ecological connectivity and viability of sensitive species. 
Entities: edge; forest patch; non-forest habitat; dispersal; migration; predation; 
competition; gene flow; transect; remote sensing; edge contrast 
Relationship Triplets: 

--- PAGE 31 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
• Edge → occurs where → forest patches meet other habitats 
• Edges → influence → dispersal and migration 
• Edges → affect → predation and competition 
• Transects → measure → variation across edges 
• Remote sensing → describes → edge length and shape 
• Hard edges → act as → barriers to organism flow 
• Soft edges → are → more permeable 
Knowledge Card 7.5.2 – Edge assessment methods and edge contrast 
Standardised protocols facilitate edge assessments. In the Swiss national forest inventory, 
forest margins within 25 m of a plot centre trigger a 50 m transect assessment recording 
floristic diversity, habitat features and aesthetic value. López-Barrera and Newton (2005) 
used 80 m × 10 m transects perpendicular to the edge, spanning 60 m inside the forest 
and 20 m into adjacent grassland; all woody plants were counted and measured to assess 
stand structure and composition. Edges are classified by contrast: ‘hard’ edges occur 
where adjacent vegetation is simple and homogeneous, creating sharp transitions, 
whereas ‘soft’ edges have structurally complex vegetation that gradually transitions into 
the forest. The degree of edge contrast influences permeability to energy, matter and 
organisms and affects interactions such as seed dispersal and predation. Proper 
replication and independence of transects are crucial to avoid pseudoreplication; studies 
often suffer from limited replication. 
Entities: Swiss national forest inventory; 50 m transect; floristic diversity; 
López-Barrera & Newton study; 80 m × 10 m transect; hard edge; soft edge; 
edge contrast; replication 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Swiss national forest inventory → assesses → forest margins using 50 m transects 
• López-Barrera & Newton study → uses → 80 m × 10 m transects across edges 
• Hard edge → has → homogeneous adjacent vegetation 
• Soft edge → has → structurally complex adjacent vegetation 
• Edge contrast → influences → permeability to organisms 
• Proper replication → avoids → pseudoreplication 
Knowledge Card 7.5.3 – Scaling and modelling edge effects 

--- PAGE 32 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
Edge effects decline with distance into habitat patches. Core area models estimate the 
portion of a patch unaffected by edge influence, based on a constant depth of edge 
influence (DEI) and are used in reserve design. Effective area models describe species 
density as a function of distance from the edge, permitting quantitative predictions across 
entire landscapes and are useful when core habitat is scarce. Determining DEI is 
challenging; typical abiotic and plant responses extend up to 50 m, invertebrate responses 
up to 100 m and bird responses 50–200 m into patches. Robust DEI estimation requires 
appropriate transect length, multiple distance categories and statistical analysis. The 
pattern of response can be positive (higher values near edges), neutral or negative (lower 
values near edges), and these patterns inform management strategies for buffering or 
promoting edge habitat. Scaling up edge effects to landscape scales remains difficult 
because most studies have low replication and limited distance categories. 
Entities: core area model; effective area model; depth of edge influence (DEI); 
abiotic responses; invertebrate responses; bird responses; distance categories; 
edge response patterns 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Core area model → identifies → patch area unaffected by edges 
• Effective area model → predicts → species density across distances from edge 
• Depth of edge influence → varies with → taxonomic group 
• Abiotic responses → extend → up to 50 m into patches 
• Invertebrate responses → extend → up to 100 m into patches 
• Bird responses → extend → up to 200 m into patches 
• Edge responses → can be → positive, neutral or negative 
Structured Summary – Edge characteristics and effects 
Edges form dynamic transition zones between forest and adjacent habitats, generating 
environmental gradients and influencing ecological processes. Transect-based surveys 
and remote sensing capture edge attributes such as length, width, shape and structural 
complexity, enabling comparisons across landscapes. Operational protocols, like those 
used in the Swiss national forest inventory, and research designs, like López-Barrera and 
Newton’s 80 m transects, demonstrate systematic approaches to measuring edge 
structure and composition. Edges are classified by contrast—hard edges mark abrupt 
transitions, whereas soft edges involve gradual structural changes—and by permeability, 
influencing flows of organisms and materials. Edge effects decay with distance, yet the 
depth of edge influence depends on the variable measured; abiotic factors attenuate within 
tens of metres, while birds may respond over hundreds of metres. Core area and effective 
area models provide frameworks for scaling edge effects and informing reserve design, 
but robust estimation requires replication, multiple distance categories and careful 
statistical analysis. Understanding edge dynamics is essential for evaluating the 

--- PAGE 33 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
consequences of fragmentation and guiding management interventions such as buffer 
zones or edge restoration. 
7.6 Habitat trees 
Knowledge Card 7.6.1 – Tree form, timber indices and veteran trees 
Tree form influences its capacity to provide habitat. Stem shape determines surface area 
available for epiphytes, while branch size, distribution and orientation create nesting and 
foraging sites. Foresters developed form factors and form quotients to estimate timber 
volume—e.g., the cylindrical form factor fc = V/(gh), where g is cross-section area at dbh 
and h is tree height—but these metrics focus on wood yield and provide limited insight into 
habitat value. In contrast, conservationists promote the concept of veteran trees, defined 
as trees of special biological, cultural or aesthetic interest because of their age, size or 
condition. The Ancient Tree Forum recognises veteran and ancient trees as synonymous 
and encourages protection of individuals displaying advanced age or features conferring 
habitat value. Such trees often occur in open environments where they develop wide 
crowns and numerous microhabitats. Understanding the limitations of timber-based form 
indices underscores the need for habitat-oriented assessments focusing on structural 
features rather than simple diameter or height measurements. 
Entities: tree form; stem shape; form factor; form quotient; cylindrical form factor (fc); 
veteran tree; Ancient Tree Forum; epiphytes; nesting sites 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Tree form → determines → surface area for epiphytes 
• Branch orientation → affects → nesting site availability 
• Form factor → estimates → stem volume for timber 
• Form quotient → relates → diameter at a given height to dbh 
• Veteran tree → is defined by → age, size or condition 
• Ancient Tree Forum → promotes → conservation of veteran trees 
• Timber indices → are limited for → habitat assessment 
Knowledge Card 7.6.2 – Habitat features of veteran and ancient trees 
Veteran trees are valued for structural features that provide habitat for diverse organisms. 
Key features include decay holes or cavities formed through limb loss and expanded by 
microorganisms and invertebrates, rot sites colonised by decay fungi, dead limbs or trunk 
sections (standing or fallen) supporting saproxylic species, hollowing of trunks or limbs, 
water pools forming in hollows, physical damage to the trunk, bark loss and sap runs, and 
deep crevices in bark or roots. The presence, size and insulation of cavities are particularly 

--- PAGE 34 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
important: up to 40 % of North American bird species nest in tree cavities, and in 
Australian eucalypt forests about 300 vertebrate species use hollows. Small entrances 
may make cavities difficult to detect, and some species use spaces between bark and 
wood rather than large hollows. These features often persist long after tree death, 
highlighting the importance of retaining snags and dead trees. Surveys must document the 
variety and condition of these microhabitats to assess habitat value effectively. 
Entities: decay hole; cavity; rot site; dead wood; hollowing; water pool; bark damage; 
sap run; crevice; cavity-nesting birds; vertebrate species; saproxylic fungi 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Decay holes → provide habitat for → cavity-nesting birds 
• Rot sites → are colonised by → decay fungi 
• Dead wood → supports → saproxylic species 
• Hollowing → creates → internal cavities 
• Water pool → forms → microhabitat for invertebrates 
• Bark damage → leads to → sap runs 
• Cavity size → affects → occupancy by birds and mammals 
• Snags → retain → habitat features after tree death 
Knowledge Card 7.6.3 – Measuring cavities and classifying standing 
dead trees 
Quantifying cavity resources requires measuring morphological variables. Gibbons et al. 
(2002) recorded minimum entrance width, minimum width at half depth, cavity depth, 
diameter of the supporting branch, branch order (with the main stem as first order), branch 
health (living, part dead, dead) and height above ground. Occupancy and species identity 
were also noted. Surveys show cavity abundance increases with tree diameter and 
decreases with the square root of tree height, reflecting more and larger hollows in older, 
larger trees. Standing dead trees (snags) are classified into decay classes: Spetich et al. 
(1999) distinguish four classes from recently dead (bark intact, twigs present) to barkless, 
heavily decayed stems, while Maser et al. (1979) define nine stages from living tree to 
decomposed stump. These classes help evaluate habitat longevity and structural integrity. 
Recognising both live and dead tree classes is essential because cavities remain valuable 
after death and different decay stages support different assemblages of organisms. 
Entities: minimum entrance width; cavity depth; branch diameter; branch order; 
branch health; tree diameter; tree height; cavity abundance; Spetich decay classes; 
Maser decay stages; occupancy 
Relationship Triplets: 

--- PAGE 35 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
• Cavity abundance → increases with → tree diameter 
• Cavity abundance → decreases with → square root of tree height 
• Branch order → categorises → cavity location 
• Branch health → affects → cavity suitability 
• Spetich decay classes → describe → stages of snag decomposition 
• Maser decay stages → range from → living tree to decomposed stump 
• Occupancy → identifies → species using cavities 
Knowledge Card 7.6.4 – Assessing tree decay and non-destructive 
techniques 
Monitoring decay status of live and dead trees combines simple and advanced techniques. 
Sounding involves striking the tree with a hammer and listening for hollow resonances; 
increment boring removes a core for visual inspection. Cordless drills with long bits can 
map decay patterns within stems. Shigometers deliver pulsed electric currents and 
measure wood tissue resistance, indicating moisture and decay status. Impedance 
tomography records resistivity patterns across stem cross-sections, providing 
non-destructive images of internal decay. These methods complement physical 
classification schemes by quantifying decay processes. Surveys should include dead trees 
because snags provide habitat even after senescence. By combining qualitative decay 
classes with quantitative measurements, researchers can assess structural integrity, 
predict failure risk and evaluate habitat value for cavity-dependent species. 
Entities: sounding; increment boring; cordless drill; shigometer; impedance tomography; 
decay status; snags; structural integrity; failure risk; non-destructive technique 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Sounding → detects → hollows via acoustic resonance 
• Increment boring → extracts → core samples for decay assessment 
• Cordless drill → maps → decay patterns inside stems 
• Shigometer → measures → tissue resistance to electric current 
• Impedance tomography → produces → resistivity images of stems 
• Snags → retain → habitat value despite decay 
• Decay status → informs → structural integrity and failure risk 
Structured Summary – Habitat trees 

--- PAGE 36 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
Trees provide diverse microhabitats, and their structural form governs the availability of 
nesting sites, cavities and substrates for epiphytes. Timber-oriented form indices, such as 
form factors and quotients, are inadequate for habitat assessments because they focus on 
wood volume rather than structural complexity. Conservationists therefore emphasise 
veteran or ancient trees—individuals of advanced age or unusual form that harbour 
numerous decay features. Habitat features of veteran trees include cavities created by 
limb loss, rot sites colonised by fungi, dead wood, hollow trunks, water-filled cavities, bark 
damage and sap runs; these microhabitats support cavity-nesting birds, bats, invertebrates 
and other taxa. Effective surveys quantify cavity dimensions, branch characteristics and 
occupancy, demonstrating that cavity abundance increases with tree diameter and 
decreases with tree height. Snags remain valuable for wildlife long after death; 
classification schemes describe stages of snag decay and should guide retention of 
standing dead trees. Assessment methods range from simple tools (soundings, increment 
borers) to advanced instruments (shigometers and impedance tomography) that 
non-destructively map decay. Integrating structural measurements with decay classes 
helps evaluate habitat value, monitor changes and inform management of tree-level 
biodiversity. 
7.7 Understory vegetation 
Knowledge Card 7.7.1 – Variables and definitions in understory 
assessment 
Understory vegetation encompasses mosses, lichens, ferns, herbaceous plants and 
shrubs. Typical variables measured include species composition, relative cover, density 
(number of plants per unit area), frequency (proportion of sample units in which a species 
occurs) and abundance (number of plants per sample). Frequency is calculated by 
recording presence or absence in multiple plots; density requires counting all individuals 
within plots, but is complicated by multi-stemmed or clonal species. Cover is defined as 
the proportion of ground surface covered by vegetation; relative cover expresses the cover 
of a species as a percentage of total vegetation cover. Visual cover estimates are widely 
used but subject to observer bias and should be cross-checked. Because vegetation is 
layered, cover can exceed 100 %, or separate estimates can be produced for each layer. 
Clear definitions and awareness of measurement limitations are essential to interpret 
understory data accurately. 
Entities: understory vegetation; species composition; relative cover; density; frequency; 
abundance; multi-stemmed species; observer bias 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Understory vegetation → includes → mosses, lichens, ferns, herbs and shrubs 
• Density → counts → number of plants per unit area 
• Frequency → measures → proportion of plots with a species present 
• Cover → expresses → proportion of ground surface occupied 

--- PAGE 37 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
• Relative cover → calculates → species cover as percent of total cover 
• Observer bias → affects → visual cover estimates 
• Multi-stemmed species → complicate → density measurement 
Knowledge Card 7.7.2 – Quadrat methods and cover estimation scales 
Understory surveys typically use small fixed-area plots (quadrats). Frame quadrats divide 
an area into subplots using wooden or metal frames; gridded quadrats facilitate recording 
cover by each species. Cover is estimated visually for each grid square or for the whole 
quadrat; when vegetation is layered, estimates may exceed 100 %. The Domin and 
Braun–Blanquet scales provide categorical cover estimates; for example, the Braun–
Blanquet scale categorises cover classes from solitary individuals (+) to 91–100 % cover 
(class 10). Photographic quadrats capture images at a constant height; a grid overlay 
allows cover estimation using the same scales. Gridded quadrats also allow calculation of 
frequency within a single quadrat (‘local frequency’) by recording presence in subplots. 
While quadrats are versatile, observer consistency and training are critical to ensure 
comparability among surveys. 
Entities: quadrat; frame quadrat; gridded quadrat; Domin scale; Braun–Blanquet scale; 
photographic quadrat; local frequency; cover classes 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Frame quadrat → divides → area into subplots 
• Gridded quadrat → facilitates → recording cover per grid square 
• Domin scale → categorises → cover into ordinal classes 
• Braun–Blanquet scale → provides → cover categories from solitary to full cover 
• Photographic quadrat → captures → images for later analysis 
• Local frequency → records → species presence in grid squares 
• Observer training → improves → consistency of cover estimation 
Knowledge Card 7.7.3 – Point quadrats, line transects and sampling 
design 
Alternative methods offer efficient sampling in different vegetation types. Point quadrats 
use thin rods (1.5–2 mm diameter) lowered vertically through vegetation; species touched 
by the rod are recorded, and cover is estimated as the proportion of hits. Point quadrats 

--- PAGE 38 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
are suitable for low, open vegetation but labour-intensive in dense stands and may miss 
understory species hidden beneath a canopy. Line transects measure the length of a 
transect intercepted by each species; percentage cover equals the ratio of intercepted 
length to total transect length. Alternatively, point transects assess species presence at 
predetermined points along a transect. Appropriate quadrat size depends on plant size and 
spacing: 0.01–0.25 m² for mosses and lichens, 0.25–16 m² for herbs and small shrubs and 
25–100 m² for tall shrubs and low trees. Sampling designs should use random or stratified 
random placement and many replicates to account for clumped distributions. 
Entities: point quadrat; line transect; point transect; quadrat size; mosses; herbs; shrubs; 
sampling design; random sampling; clumped distribution 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Point quadrat → records → species touched by a rod 
• Line transect → measures → interception length of species 
• Point transect → assesses → species presence at specific points 
• Quadrat size → depends on → plant size and spacing 
• Random sampling → selects → quadrat locations without bias 
• Clumped distribution → requires → many replicates to capture variability 
Structured Summary – Understory vegetation 
Assessment of understory vegetation provides essential information about species 
composition and structure. Key variables include species composition, relative cover, 
density, frequency, abundance and size. Cover estimates express the fraction of ground 
covered by vegetation and may exceed 100 % when layers overlap; relative cover 
standardises estimates for comparison. Frequency records the proportion of plots 
occupied by a species, while density counts individuals within plots but can be complicated 
by clonal growth forms. Fixed-area quadrats, including frame and gridded designs, enable 
systematic cover and frequency estimates; categorical scales such as Domin and Braun–
Blanquet standardise visual cover assessments. Photographic quadrats provide 
permanent records for analysis. Point quadrats and line transects offer alternative 
methods: point quadrats count species hits along vertical rods, and line transects measure 
the intercept length along a horizontal line. Appropriate quadrat sizes depend on 
vegetation stature, and random or stratified placement with sufficient replication is 
essential to sample patchy distributions. Clear definitions and standardised protocols 
enhance comparability of understory surveys across sites and times. 
7.8 Habitat models 
Knowledge Card 7.8.1 – Habitat modelling approaches and process 

--- PAGE 39 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
Habitat models translate ecological knowledge into spatial predictions of species 
distributions and habitat quality. Forest managers require such maps for planning 
conservation and sustainable use. Elith and Burgman (2003) and Guisan and 
Zimmermann (2000) outline seven modelling approaches, ranging from simple conceptual 
models to complex statistical and machine-learning methods. The modelling process 
includes: (1) conceptual model based on expert knowledge or field observations; (2) 
statistical formulation, specifying predictor variables and functional forms; (3) calibration 
using a fitting dataset; (4) prediction to unmeasured locations; and (5) evaluation, 
assessing predictive power with independent or bootstrap datasets. Figure 7.16 illustrates 
this workflow, emphasising separate calibration and evaluation datasets and techniques 
like cross-validation and ROC curves for presence–absence models. Habitat models may 
incorporate field data, literature, laboratory experiments and existing models, synthesising 
multiple information sources. By explicitly documenting assumptions and data sources, 
modelling frameworks help managers identify knowledge gaps and prioritise data 
collection. 
Entities: habitat model; conceptual model; statistical formulation; calibration; prediction; 
evaluation; modelling approach; predictor variables; cross-validation; ROC curve 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Habitat model → generates → habitat maps for species 
• Conceptual model → forms → the foundation of the modelling process 
• Statistical formulation → specifies → predictor variables and relationships 
• Calibration → fits → the model to data 
• Prediction → applies → the model to unmeasured locations 
• Evaluation → assesses → predictive performance 
• Cross-validation → evaluates → model accuracy 
Knowledge Card 7.8.2 – Expert-based conceptual models and the HSI 
approach 
Expert opinion is often used when empirical data are scarce. Key challenges include 
selecting competent experts, eliciting information and aggregating responses. Methods for 
elicitation include questionnaires, interviews and structured group discussions, while 
aggregation techniques such as the Delphi method compute summary statistics and iterate 
feedback. Disagreement among experts is addressed by expressing uncertainty with 
subjective probability distributions and weighting expert opinions. Conceptual models take 
the form of diagrams or influence networks showing variables, parameters and causal 
connections. The Habitat Suitability Index (HSI) is a widely used expert-based model that 
identifies critical habitat components for a species (e.g., food, cover and breeding sites) 
and assigns a suitability value from 0 (poor) to 1 (optimal) for each component. The overall 
HSI is derived by combining individual suitability indices using additive, multiplicative or 

--- PAGE 40 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
logical functions. HSI models integrate expert knowledge into simple frameworks but rely 
on subjective judgements and may need refinement as empirical data become available. 
Entities: expert opinion; elicitation method; Delphi technique; conceptual model; 
influence diagram; Habitat Suitability Index (HSI); suitability index; uncertainty; 
subjective probability 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Expert opinion → informs → conceptual models 
• Elicitation methods → include → questionnaires and interviews 
• Delphi technique → aggregates → expert responses 
• Conceptual model → depicts → variables and causal connections 
• HSI → assigns → suitability values to habitat components 
• Suitability indices → are combined into → overall HSI 
• Uncertainty → is expressed by → subjective probability distributions 
Knowledge Card 7.8.3 – Geographic envelope models 
Geographic envelope models describe species’ geographic ranges using point location 
data. Convex hulls create the smallest convex polygon that contains all known occurrence 
points; this method is recommended by IUCN for estimating extent of occurrence, but its 
coarse resolution can overestimate range. α-hulls (alpha hulls) generalise convex hulls by 
constructing polygons from a Delaunay triangulation with a parameter controlling the 
maximum internal angle; they better capture irregularly shaped ranges. Kernel density 
estimators place a smoothing kernel over each observation and sum kernels to estimate 
occurrence density; they are widely used in home-range analysis and can delineate 
species ranges. The choice of envelope model depends on data quality, species ecology 
and management objectives. Envelope models provide rapid approximations of range 
boundaries but may ignore environmental suitability; therefore, they are often 
complemented by environmental niche models that integrate habitat variables. 
Entities: geographic envelope model; convex hull; α-hull; Delaunay triangulation; 
kernel density estimator; extent of occurrence; species range; IUCN 
Relationship Triplets: 
• Geographic envelope model → describes → species range using point data 
• Convex hull → encloses → all occurrence points within a convex polygon 
• α-hull → generalises → convex hull by limiting internal angles 

--- PAGE 41 ---
Interdisziplinäre Projektstudien 
Task 3
Forest Ecology & 
Biodiversity
Noah Glasgow 
11927988
• Kernel density estimator → places → smoothing kernels over points to estimate 
density 
• IUCN → recommends → convex hull for extent of occurrence 
• Envelope models → may overestimate → species range when convex 
• Envelope models → lack → environmental suitability information 
Structured Summary – Habitat models 
Habitat models are essential tools for conservation planning and ecological research, 
translating data and expert knowledge into spatial predictions of species distributions and 
habitat quality. The modelling process involves conceptualising the system, formalising 
statistical relationships, calibrating models with data, making spatial predictions and 
evaluating model performance using independent datasets or resampling techniques. 
Expert-based conceptual models leverage knowledge from local people, researchers and 
managers; elicitation methods must address expert selection, aggregation and uncertainty. 
The Habitat Suitability Index (HSI) approach identifies key habitat components and 
assigns suitability scores that are combined to map habitat quality. In parallel, geographic 
envelope models delineate species ranges using spatial point data; convex hulls provide 
coarse range estimates, α-hulls capture irregular shapes and kernel density estimators 
produce smoothed range surfaces. While envelope models yield rapid range 
approximations, they lack environmental context and are best used alongside models 
incorporating habitat variables. Collectively, these approaches support decision-making by 
revealing knowledge gaps, guiding data collection and informing prioritisation of 
conservation efforts.

Knowledge Card 4.1.2 – Environmental stresses and frost hardiness 
Mortality in trees depends not only on density but also on environmental stresses such as 
frost, drought, insects and diseases. Modelling mortality mechanistically is difficult because 
stress effects are complex and data are scarce. Frost hardiness refers to the capacity of 
leaves to withstand freezing; acclimation increases hardiness during cold nights and 
declines under warm conditions. King & Ball’s study on Eucalyptus pauciflora showed that 
frost events can cause both rapid recovery and chronic effects that reduce photosynthesis; 
long-term reductions lead to carbon starvation and enhanced mortality. Frost damage may 
kill expanding shoots, delaying leaf regeneration. Incorporating frost hardiness into 
mortality models requires quantifying acclimation and recovery, a challenge seldom 
achieved.

Knowledge Card 4.1.3 – Drought-induced mortality and physiological 
mechanisms 
Drought-induced mortality has a strong physiological basis. Suarez et al. found mortality in 
Nothofagus dombeyi results from additive effects of ontogeny, vigour, climatic sensitivity 
and physiology. McDowell et al. proposed a hydraulic framework where drought mortality 
depends on (i) carbon starvation – prolonged drought reduces photosynthesis, exhausting 
carbohydrate reserves; (ii) hydraulic failure – severe drought causes irreversible xylem 
cavitation and desiccation; and (iii) biotic agents – insects or pathogens whose impacts are 
amplified by carbon starvation or hydraulic failure. They distinguished isohydric species 
(e.g., piñon pine) that maintain constant leaf water potential by closing stomata and incur 
reduced photosynthesis versus anisohydric species (e.g., juniper) that allow water 
potential to decline and are susceptible to hydraulic failure. Thus species’ water-regulation 
strategies mediate drought mortality. 
Entities: drought-induced mortality, carbon starvation, hydraulic failure, biotic agents, 
photosynthesis < respiration, xylem cavitation, isohydric species, constant leaf water 
potential, anisohydric species, declining water potential, reduced photosynthesis, mortality 
from biotic agents 
Relationship Triplets: 
• drought-induced mortality → results from → carbon starvation 
• drought-induced mortality → results from → hydraulic failure 
• drought-induced mortality → results from → biotic agents

• hydraulic failure → involves → xylem cavitation

Knowledge Card 4.1.4 – Self-thinning processes in even-aged stands 
Following canopy closure, high stem densities lead to density-dependent mortality known 
as self-thinning. Early regeneration produces numerous seedlings; competition for light 
and resources results in some trees dominating while suppressed individuals die. 
Self-thinning is described by empirical power-law relationships between maximum average 
stem mass  and population density . Equation (4.5)  has 
an exponent  and parameter  as stem mass at 1000 trees ha-1. As 
self-thinning progresses, stand basal area increases to a maximum and stem numbers 
decline along a self-thinning line. Alternative models use differential equations linking stem 
number and DBH to basal area. Self-thinning is initiated when average stem mass 
exceeds the maximum allowable mass for the current density; subsequent mortality aligns 
the stand with the self-thinning trajectory. 
Entities: canopy closure, self-thinning, stem number, stand basal area, self-thinning line, 
self-thinning exponent, maximum average stem mass, stem density, suppressed trees, 
density-dependent mortality, dominant trees, resources after thinning 
Relationship Triplets: 
• canopy closure → precedes → self-thinning 
• self-thinning → reduces → stem number 
• self-thinning → increases → stand basal area 
• self-thinning line → describes → relationship between and 
• self-thinning exponent → approximately equals → 1.5 
• maximum average stem mass → depends on → stem density 
• suppressed trees → experience → density-dependent mortality 
• dominant trees → capture → resources after thinning

Structured Summary 4.1 – Stem Population Dynamics 
Even-aged plantations exhibit predictable dynamics in stem populations. At establishment, 
dense stands undergo high seedling mortality; subsequent stability is followed by 
density-dependent mortality when competition intensifies. Mortality rate functions vary with 
age, site and management and can be modelled as exponential declines with 
age-dependent parameters. Environmental stresses modulate mortality; frost acclimation 
and drought physiology demonstrate that mortality is not solely density driven. Frost 
hardiness depends on acclimation and influences photosynthesis, while drought-induced 
mortality arises from carbon starvation, hydraulic failure and biotic interactions. Isohydric 
versus anisohydric water regulation strategies determine species-specific risk. After

canopy closure, self-thinning governs stand structure: a power-law relation between 
maximum stem mass and population density defines a self-thinning line along which basal 
area peaks while stem numbers decline. Differential equations incorporating DBH and 
basal area describe the transition to this line. Allometric relations between stem mass and 
diameter underpin self-thinning exponents, linking biomass scaling with population 
dynamics. Thus stem population dynamics integrate demographic processes, 
environmental responses and size-dependent relationships, forming the basis for growth 
models and management in even-aged forests.

Tree height and stem diameter are tightly linked through biomechanical constraints. The 
principle of elastic similarity states that the critical height  beyond which a stem 
becomes mechanically unstable scales with diameter: , 
where  is the elastic modulus of green wood,  the wood density and . With 
typical values ( ), the critical height for a 100 cm DBH tree 
is roughly 185 m; in practice trees limit height to about a quarter of  due to wind forces 
and gravitational loads. Elastic similarity applies to branches and influences crown shape. 
Site factors such as wind exposure, shading and crowding affect stem slenderness and 
elasticity; slender stems show higher elastic modulus values.

Knowledge Card 4.2.2 – Allometric height–diameter relation 
incorporating stand density  
Beyond mechanical constraints, height–diameter relations reflect competition. An empirical 
allometric model expresses mean tree height  as , where  is 
a scale factor,  the exponent for diameter and  the exponent for stem population 
density . This relation recognises that closely spaced trees allocate more biomass to 
height growth, giving taller slender stems. Data from spacing trials with Eucalyptus grandis 
clones showed  and  with high goodness of fit. Site and clone 
variations influence  and exponents; slender stems from sites with high wind or 
competition have greater elastic modulus but lower diameter growth. As trees age, 
biomass allocation shifts from height growth to structural support, causing exponents to 
decrease.

Knowledge Card 4.2.5 – Age dependence and applications of height–
diameter models 
Height–diameter and biomass relations vary with stand age and stand structure. Allometric 
parameters  and  fitted to age-specific data differ from those derived from age-series 
data. Relationships based on a single age are used to estimate biomass at that age, 
whereas age-series relationships describe how stand properties vary over time. Age 
affects the allometric exponent: young trees allocate more to height, giving higher 
exponents; as trees age, exponents decline toward elastic similarity values. Height and 
diameter distributions evolve over time, and Weibull parameters can be modelled as 
functions of stand age, basal area and mean height. The choice of allometric relationship 
depends on modelling purpose – stand-level predictions (e.g., 3-PG) versus individual tree 
biomass estimation.

specific time, age-series relationships, temporal variation of stand properties, young trees, 
more biomass to height, ageing trees, diameter and support tissues, Weibull parameters, 
age and basal area, 3-PG model, stand-level allometric relationships, individual tree 
models, age-specific allometric equations

Structured Summary 4.2 – Height and Diameter Relations and 
Distributions 
Tree structural characteristics are governed by biomechanical constraints, allometric 
relations and statistical distributions. Mechanical stability imposes an upper limit on height 
that scales with diameter: the critical height formula predicts a maximum height 
proportional to  and dependent on wood stiffness and density. Trees usually attain only 
a quarter of this height, reflecting adaptive safety margins. Height–diameter relations are 
further modified by competition: an allometric expression  
captures how densely stocked stands promote height growth at the expense of diameter; 
exponents and scale factors vary among species, clones and sites. Predicting stem 
biomass from basal area and height is accomplished through dimensional relations 
; empirical regressions provide robust estimates across species. Stem size 
distributions follow Weibull functions whose parameters capture skewness and change 
with age. Age influences allometric parameters; young trees allocate more to height, while 
older trees shift to structural support, causing exponents to decline. Modelling applications 
must therefore select age-appropriate relations: age-specific functions for individual-tree 
biomass or age-series functions for stand-level dynamics. Integrating mechanical theory, 
allometric scaling and statistical distributions enables the conversion of process-based 
model outputs into practical forestry variables.

Implications

scaling equations

As trees grow, their components change size in a coordinated but not strictly geometric 
fashion. Isometric scaling preserves shape; areas scale with length squared and volumes 
with length cubed. However, plant growth often departs from isometry; such departures are 
termed allometry. Two fundamental equations describe allometry: biomass of a component 
 scales with total plant biomass  as  (Eq. 4.20), and a plant attribute  (e.g., 
height or biomass pool) scales with a measure of size  as  (Eq. 4.21). In 
isometry,  when both variables have the same dimension, while allometric 
exponents differ from unity. Log-log plots linearize these power laws, but parameter 
estimates of  and  are often correlated. To compare clones or treatments meaningfully, it 
is advisable to fix the exponent  across data sets and allow the scale factor  to vary.

Knowledge Card 4.3.2 – Allometric relationships among biomass pools 
Allometric scaling between biomass pools reveals how plants allocate mass. Studies on 
Pinus sylvestris (age 7–100 years) and Eucalyptus grandis (age ≤7 years) show that main 
stem biomass and total tree biomass follow power laws with exponents  and 
 respectively. Coarse root biomass also scales allometrically but with different 
exponents (1.20 for pine and 0.85 for eucalypt). Functional pools like branches and foliage 
exhibit weak or no allometric relationship because they vary dynamically with environment 
and litterfall. These differences illustrate that structural components (stems and coarse 
roots) maintain relatively stable proportions across growth conditions, whereas functional 
components respond plastically. The robustness of these relationships across species, 
ages and spacings supports their use in constraining biomass allocation in growth models.

Knowledge Card 4.3.4 – Age effects on allometric parameters and stand-
level versus individual relationships  
Allometric parameters change with tree age and vary between individual-tree and stand-
level analyses. Age-specific allometric equations fitted to trees of a common age yield 
parameters  and  that differ from those fitted across an age series. In Pinus sylvestris 
stands aged 24, 50 and 87 years, the exponent  declines slightly with age while the scale 
factor  increases, indicating that older trees require thicker stems per unit diameter. 
Stand-level mean biomass and DBH relations differ from individual-tree relations: using 
mean values yields distinct parameters because the distribution of tree sizes and biomass 
influences the mean relation. This has practical implications—process-based models like 
3-PG use stand-level allometric relationships to constrain biomass allocation, whereas 
individual-tree biomass estimation relies on age-specific equations. Choosing the correct 
relationship is essential for accurate predictions.

• tree size distribution → affects → mean allometric parameters

Knowledge Card 4.3.5 – Species, clone and environmental effects on 
allometric relationships  
Allometry varies not only with age but also with genetics and environment. In 
Eucalyptus grandis clone trials, fitting Eq. (4.21) to stem and coarse root biomass across 
five clones (C1–C5) showed significant differences in scale factors; e.g., clones C2 and C3 
had similar stem scale factors but differed for roots, while clones C2 and C5 formed 
extremes for root biomass. Stem number, fertility and water status also affect allometry. 
Spacing and irrigation experiments with E. grandis (spacing 1111–10 000 trees ha-1) 
demonstrated that variations in stem number and DBH dominate biomass and height 
variation, whereas water availability had minor effects except on height and branch/bark 
proportion. Allometric relations for functional pools (foliage, fine roots) are highly sensitive 
to environmental conditions and may display weak or no correlations. Thus growth models 
should incorporate species-specific and site-specific allometric parameters and adjust for 
stem density and resource availability. 
Entities: clone differences, allometric scale factors, clone C2 and C3, stem scale factor, 
clone C2 and C5, root scale factor, stem number, allometric relationships, fertility, biomass 
partitioning, water availability, height and branch/bark ratio, functional biomass pools, 
environmental conditions, growth models, species- and site-specific allometry 
Relationship Triplets: 
• clone differences → affect → allometric scale factors 
• clone C2 and C3 → have similar → stem scale factor 
• clone C2 and C5 → represent extremes in → root scale factor 
• stem number → influences → allometric relationships 
• fertility → affects → biomass partitioning 
• water availability → modestly influences → height and branch/bark ratio 
• functional biomass pools → sensitive to → environmental conditions 
• growth models → should incorporate → species- and site-specific allometry 
Structured Summary 4.3 – Allometric Scaling and Its Implications 
Allometric scaling describes how tree attributes change relative to overall size. Perfect 
geometric similarity (isometry) is rare; instead, power-law relations with exponents differing 
from unity characterize biomass allocation and size relationships. Fundamental equations 
express component biomass or plant attributes as , where  is a scale factor and 
 an exponent. Main stems and coarse roots show strong allometric relations to total 
biomass, with exponents around 1.17–1.23 for stems and species-specific values for roots.

11927988
Functional components (branches, foliage) exhibit weak scaling due to dynamic responses 
and turnover. Using DBH as the size measure yields robust predictions of stem and root 
biomass; exponents for stem biomass around 2.87 apply across species, but foliage 
relations are weak. Allometric parameters shift with age: older trees have larger scale 
factors and slightly reduced exponents, and stand-level means differ from individual-tree 
relationships. Genetics and environment further modulate allometry; clones differ in scale 
factors, stem density influences height–diameter relations, and resource availability affects 
functional pools. Hence allometric equations must be tailored to species, age and site 
conditions when constraining biomass allocation or deriving stand variables from process-
based models. 
Conceptual Unit 4.4 – Leaf Area of Trees and Canopies 
Knowledge Card 4.4.1 – Canopy architecture and its determinants 
Forest canopy architecture influences photosynthesis and transpiration. A canopy consists 
of the crowns of trees; its vertical and horizontal arrangement of foliage determines how 
much photosynthetically active radiation (PAR) is intercepted. Canopy roughness also 
affects aerodynamic coupling with the atmosphere. Tree-level factors such as crown 
shape, genetic traits and environmental conditions (latitude, moisture) produce crowns 
ranging from narrow, columnar forms at high latitudes or dry sites to broad, spherical 
shapes at low latitudes or well-watered sites. Stand-level determinants include stem 
number, spacing, height and size distribution, as well as soil fertility and precipitation. 
Deciduous canopies change dramatically during the growing season as foliage emerges 
and falls; evergreen canopies change more slowly, depending on leaf longevity. 
Entities: canopy architecture, PAR interception, transpiration rate, crown shape, latitude 
and moisture, stand spacing, crown breadth, soil fertility, canopy structure, deciduous 
canopies, seasonal change, evergreen canopies, longer leaf longevity, vertical and 
horizontal foliage distribution, canopy roughness 
Relationship Triplets: 
• canopy architecture → determines → PAR interception 
• canopy architecture → determines → transpiration rate 
• crown shape → varies with → latitude and moisture 
• stand spacing → influences → crown breadth 
• soil fertility → affects → canopy structure 
• deciduous canopies → undergo → seasonal change 
• evergreen canopies → have → longer leaf longevity

Knowledge Card 4.4.2 – Specific leaf area (SLA) and its variability  
Specific leaf area (SLA) is the projected leaf area per unit foliage mass (m² kg⁻¹). SLA links 
leaf area  and foliage mass  via . At the canopy level, leaf area index 
(LAI) equals 0.1 s_F W_F where W_F is stand foliage mass (t ha-1). SLA varies with 
species, leaf age, canopy position, nutrition and light. Conifers typically have SLA 4–
5 m² kg⁻¹; mature eucalypts 4–8 m² kg⁻¹; young eucalypts 10–20 m² kg⁻¹; and broad-leaved 
deciduous trees around 10 m² kg⁻¹. SLA decreases with increasing solar radiation and 
increases with higher leaf nitrogen concentration. Shade leaves generally have higher SLA 
than sun leaves, and SLA declines as stands age because juvenile foliage with high SLA is 
gradually replaced by mature foliage with lower SLA. 
Entities: specific leaf area, leaf area, foliage mass, leaf area index LAI, conifer species, 
mature eucalypts, young eucalypts, broad-leaved deciduous trees, solar radiation, leaf 
nitrogen concentration 
Relationship Triplets: 
• specific leaf area  → links → leaf area  to foliage mass  
• leaf area index LAI → equals → 0.1  
• conifer species → have → SLA ≈ 4–5 m² kg⁻¹ 
• mature eucalypts → have → SLA ≈ 4–8 m² kg⁻¹ 
• young eucalypts → have → SLA ≈ 10–20 m² kg⁻¹ 
• broad-leaved deciduous trees → have → SLA ≈ 10 m² kg⁻¹ 
• SLA → decreases with → increasing solar radiation 
• SLA → increases with → leaf nitrogen concentration

Knowledge Card 4.4.4 – Estimating leaf area from stem diameter and 
sapwood area  
Directly measuring leaf area is destructive, but leaf area per tree can be estimated 
indirectly. Foliage mass  often scales with DBH via ; the coefficients vary 
seasonally in deciduous species and with density, age and nutrition in evergreens. 
Alternatively, foliage mass is linearly related to sapwood cross-sectional area  at the 
base of the crown: . Combining these relationships with SLA allows 
estimation of leaf area  from DBH or sapwood area via . The sapwood–
foliage relation reflects a physiological link: a unit mass of foliage is serviced by a specific 
cross-sectional area of conducting tissue. Drier environments have lower leaf area per unit 
sapwood area, indicating adaptation to reduce transpiration. Non-destructive instruments 
measuring light transmission through canopies can also estimate LAI.

Knowledge Card 4.4.5 – Calculating and modelling leaf area index (LAI) 
 
Canopy LAI () is the cumulative leaf area per unit ground area and is calculated by 
integrating foliage area density  over canopy height: . In practice, 
discrete height zones approximate the integral. LAI is key for light interception and water 
use. Given stand density and size distribution (e.g., from a Weibull diameter distribution),  
LAI can be calculated by summing leaf area across tree size classes using relationships 
between foliage mass and DBH and SLA. Accurate light-interception modelling may 
require information on foliage distribution and clumping. Non-forestry canopies use 
sampling and biomass partitioning to estimate LAI. LAI often reaches about 3 at canopy 
closure; at this point 78% of incident radiation is intercepted (with extinction coefficient 
).

and size distribution, LAI calculation, Weibull diameter distribution, tree size classes, 
canopy closure, LAI ≈ 3, extinction coefficient , light interception, clumping of foliage, 
radiation penetration, non-forestry canopies, sampling and partitioning

Knowledge Card 4.4.6 – Modelling closed-canopy LAI and site/
physiological factors  
Closed-canopy LAI depends on environmental factors and physiological parameters. 
Battaglia & Sands (1997) modelled the closed-canopy LAI  as the minimum of nutrition-
limited (), temperature-limited () and water-limited () indices. For 
Eucalyptus globulus plantations, the maximum LAI ( ) and parameter sets 
produce curves relating LAI to mean annual temperature and a water-stress indicator. 
They also introduced a physiologically based formula where closed-canopy LAI maximises 
net photosynthesis: . Parameters include canopy 
light extinction coefficient , specific leaf area , light-use efficiency , annual incident 
PAR , maintenance respiration per unit nitrogen , leaf nitrogen concentration  and 
litterfall rate . The model performs well when litterfall rate  increases on cold or dry 
sites. These approaches illustrate how simple empirical and physiological models can 
predict LAI from site variables and leaf physiology.

Knowledge Card 4.4.7 – Foliage distribution, dynamics and litterfall 
modelling 
The spatial arrangement and temporal dynamics of foliage influence canopy function. 
Canopies may be homogeneous with foliage uniformly distributed between canopy base 
and top, or clumped within crowns, causing gaps and uneven light interception. Foliage 
distribution affects where radiation is absorbed: clumped canopies absorb less in upper 
layers but more in lower layers. Evergreen and deciduous stands have different vertical 
foliage density profiles (illustrated in Figure 4.8). Foliage mass changes over time: 
increases until canopy closure and stabilises as litterfall balances growth; eventually 
declines as allocation shifts from foliage to stem. Environmental factors, particularly water 
supply, profoundly affect litterfall patterns. Experiments on Pinus radiata showed drought 
leads to elevated litterfall and reduces LAI, while irrigation reduces needle loss. Litterfall 
can be modelled by , where  is the fraction of net production 
allocated to foliage and  is the litterfall rate. To capture seasonal dynamics, litterfall rate 
 is expressed as a function of water stress and temperature. Fine-scale models, like 
those in 3-PG, use logistic functions to represent litterfall through stand development and 
modify rates in response to stress. 
Entities: homogeneous canopy, uniform foliage distribution, clumped canopy, gaps in 
foliage, foliage distribution, radiation absorption profile, foliage mass, canopy closure, 
litterfall rate , foliage growth after closure, drought conditions, litterfall, irrigation, needle 
loss, litterfall model, foliage dynamics 
Relationship Triplets: 
• homogeneous canopy → has → uniform foliage distribution 
• clumped canopy → creates → gaps in foliage

Structured Summary – Leaf Area and Canopy Processes 
Forest canopies are complex structures whose architecture, foliage traits and dynamics 
govern light interception and water use. Crown shape and canopy roughness vary with 
genetic and environmental factors, while stand variables such as spacing and size 
distribution determine overall canopy architecture. The specific leaf area (SLA) links 
foliage mass to leaf area and varies widely across species, ages and canopy positions; it 
decreases with increasing light and increases with nitrogen, with juvenile leaves displaying 
higher SLA than mature leaves. SLA transitions through stand development can be 
modelled with a sigmoidal function controlled by juvenile and mature values, transition age 
and rate. Leaf area per tree is estimated indirectly from DBH or sapwood area combined 
with SLA, reflecting physiological coupling between conductive tissue and foliage. Canopy 
LAI is the integral of foliage area density and can be computed from stand density and size 
distributions; LAI around 3 marks canopy closure and intercepts roughly 78% of incident 
radiation. Empirical and physiological models predict closed-canopy LAI based on 
nutrition, temperature, water availability and leaf physiology. Foliage distribution and 
dynamics further modulate canopy function: homogeneous versus clumped distributions 
alter light penetration, and temporal changes in foliage mass reflect growth, litterfall and 
environmental stress. Litterfall modelling uses allocation and decay rates modified by 
water and temperature stress to capture seasonal variation. Together, these concepts 
integrate leaf area, canopy architecture and environmental drivers, enabling models to 
predict photosynthesis, transpiration and productivity across diverse forests. 
Conceptual Unit 4.5 – Roots 
Knowledge Card 4.5.1 – Functions, trade-offs and environmental 
influences on root systems  
Tree root systems provide anchorage and absorb water and nutrients. Adequate support 
requires roots that spread widely and deeply; efficient absorption requires a large surface 
area of fine roots. These requirements may conflict: in wet soils with high winds, even 
extensive roots may not anchor trees sufficiently; in dry soils a fibrous root system with 
many fine roots may be disadvantageous because high resistance to water flow limits 
water transport. Root structure varies by species (genetically controlled) but is modified by

environmental conditions such as soil moisture and fertility; roots proliferate in nutrient-rich 
and moist zones but avoid waterlogged or dry soils. Root systems adjust dynamically: 
under favourable conditions assimilates shift to above-ground growth, while nutrient or 
water shortage diverts assimilates to increase root surface area. Thus the balance 
between root and shoot activity depends on resource availability.

Knowledge Card 4.5.2 – Root classification and estimation of fine root 
mass 
To quantify roots, Jackson & Chittenden (1981) excavated Pinus radiata roots (aged 3–
8 years) and classified them into classes: very large (>20 mm), large (10–20 mm), medium  
(5–10 mm), small (2–5 mm) and fine (<2 mm). Consistency in classification ensures 
comparability across studies. Regression equations allow estimation of fine root dry weight 
 from small root weight  or foliage weight :  and 
. Fine root mass varies most within a short time because fine 
roots are transient, whereas larger support roots are more persistent. These relationships 
reflect functional balance: small roots provide structure for fine roots, and foliage supplies 
assimilates to produce them.

Knowledge Card 4.5.4 – Root dynamics: production and turnover  
Root dynamics involve production and turnover of fine and coarse roots. Santantonio 
(1989) found that fine root biomass correlates with foliage biomass and is site-quality 
dependent: fine root mass per unit foliage is roughly three times higher on poor sites 
compared to good sites. Ford & Deans (1977) observed no net increase in fine root weight 
of Sitka spruce over a growing season, though fine root density varied with time. Deans 
(1981) reported that the youngest roots (>5 mm) grew fastest and contributed most to root 
biomass; root production of trees aged ~10 years was about 8 t ha⁻1 yr⁻1, representing 
~70% of annual production. Grier et al. (1981) showed that below-ground production 
accounts for 65–73% of primary production in young and mature Abies amabilis stands. 
Root growth often precedes shoot growth, capturing resources before new leaves expand. 
These studies highlight the significant carbon investment in root turnover and suggest that 
a larger fraction of assimilates is allocated below ground on nutrient-poor sites. 
Entities: fine root biomass, foliage biomass, poor sites, higher fine root biomass per 
foliage, fine root density, season, young roots (>5 mm), root biomass, root production, 8 t 
ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ for 10-year-old spruce, below-ground production, 65–73% of total production, root 
growth, shoot growth, nutrient-poor sites, allocation to fine roots 
Relationship Triplets: 
• fine root biomass → correlates with → foliage biomass 
• poor sites → have → higher fine root biomass per foliage 
• fine root density → varies with → season 
• young roots (>5 mm) → grow faster and contribute most to → root biomass 
• root production → ≈ → 8 t ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ for 10-year-old spruce 
• below-ground production → accounts for → 65–73% of total production 
• root growth → precedes → shoot growth 
• nutrient-poor sites → increase → allocation to fine roots
